Stokes waves at the critical depth are modulational unstable
Massimiliano Berti, Alberto Maspero, Paolo Ventura

Abstract
This paper fully answers a long standing open question concerning the stability/instability of pure gravity periodic traveling water waves –called Stokes waves– at the critical Whitham-Benjamin depth 𝚑 WB = 1.363 ​ … \mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}=1.363... and nearby values. We prove that Stokes waves of small amplitude 𝒪 ⁡ ( ϵ ) \mathcal{O}(\epsilon) are, at the critical depth 𝚑 WB \mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}} , linearly unstable under long wave perturbations. This is also true for slightly smaller values of the depth 𝚑 > 𝚑 WB − c ​ ϵ 2 \mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}-c\epsilon^{2} , c > 0 c>0 , depending on the amplitude of the wave. This problem was not rigorously solved in previous literature because the expansions degenerate at the critical depth. In order to resolve this degenerate case, and describe in a mathematically exhaustive way how the eigenvalues change their stable-to-unstable nature along this shallow-to-deep water transient, we Taylor expand the computations of [ 10 ] at a higher degree of accuracy, derived by the fourth order expansion of the Stokes waves. We prove that also in this transient regime a pair of unstable eigenvalues depict a closed figure 8, of smaller size than for 𝚑 > 𝚑 WB \mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}} , as the Floquet exponent varies.



1
Introduction and main result

In the last years substantial mathematical progresses have been obtained in the classical problem of determining the stability/instability of the Stokes waves, i.e. periodic traveling waves of the gravity water waves equations in any depth,
subject to long wave perturbations.

Let us briefly summarize the state of the art.
The existence of small amplitude
Stokes waves, pioneered by the famous work of Stokes
[
43
]
in 1847,
was first rigorously proved by Struik
[
46
]
, Levi-Civita
[
36
]
,
and Nekrasov
[
39
]
one century ago, and then extended
to global branches containing extreme
waves in
[
33
,
47
,
38
,
17
,
1
,
41
]
.
In the sixties Benjamin and Feir
[
4
,
3
]
, Whitham
[
48
]
,
Lighthill
[
37
]
and Zakharov
[
51
,
53
]

discovered, through experiments and formal arguments, that small amplitude
Stokes waves in
sufficiently deep water are unstable, proposing a heuristic mechanism which leads
to the disintegration of wave trains.
More precisely, these works predicted the existence of a critical depth
–that we shall call the
Whitham-Benjamin depth
–

\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}:=1.363...\,,

such that
2\pi\kappa
-space periodic Stokes waves in an ocean of depth

\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}\kappa^{-1}
are unstable: in correspondence with
small Floquet exponents
\mu
(i.e. long-wave perturbations)
the linearized equations
at the Stokes wave possess
a pair of
eigenvalues with non-zero real part close to zero.
This phenomenon is nowadays called
“Benjamin-Feir” –or modulational– instability, and it is supported by an enormous amount of physical observations and numerical simulations, see e.g.
[
20
,
34
,
21
]
.
We refer to
[
54
]
for a historical survey of the modulational theory of wave packets
for several dispersive and fluid PDE models.
We remark that modulational instability has indeed been observed
also in a variety of approximate water waves models, such as KdV, gKdV, NLS and the Whitham equation, see
[
49
,
42
,
24
,
25
,
14
,
30
,
26
,
13
,
27
,
35
]
.

For the water waves equations, the first mathematically
rigorous proof of a local branch of unstable Benjamin-Feir eigenvalues close to zero
for
\kappa\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}

was obtained by
Bridges-Mielke
[
11
]
in finite depth (see also Hur-Yang

[
28
]
)
via
a center manifold reduction,
and recently by Nguyen-Strauss
[
40
]

via a Lyapunov-Schmidt decomposition. In deep water we mention the nonlinear modulational result by Chen-Su
[
15
]
.

Very recently Berti-Maspero-Ventura
[
7
,
9
]
developed a
completely different rigorous spectral approach,
based on a symplectic version of Kato’s theory of similarity transformations
and a block diagonalization technique inspired by KAM theory,
which provided the full topological
splitting of all the four eigenvalues close to zero
as the Floquet exponent
\mu
is turned on. More precisely the works

[
7
,
9
]
,
that, with no loss of generality, are formulated for

2\pi
-periodic Stokes waves, i.e. with wave number
\kappa=1
,
rigorously prove that:

•

Shallow water case:
for any
0<\mathtt{h}<\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}

the eigenvalues close to zero
are purely imaginary for Stokes waves
of sufficiently small amplitude
\epsilon
, see Figure
1
-left;

•

Sufficiently deep water case:

for any
\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}<\mathtt{h}\leq\infty
,
there exists a pair of
eigenvalues with non-zero real part, which
traces a complete closed figure “8” (as shown in Figure
1
-right) parameterized by the Floquet exponent
\mu
.
As
{\mathtt{h}}\to\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}^{\,+}
the set of unstable Floquet exponents shrinks to zero and the Benjamin-Feir unstable eigenvalues
collapse to the origin
(we remark that
the case

\mathtt{h}=+\infty

is not a consequence of the finite depth case).
The figure 8 had been numerically observed in Deconinck-Oliveras
[
20
]
.

(a)

*

[.4]

(b)

*

[.4]

Figure 1
:
The left picture shows that for
\mathtt{h}<\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
the eigenvalues
\lambda^{\pm}_{1}(\mu,\epsilon)
and
\lambda^{\pm}_{0}(\mu,\epsilon)
are purely imaginary.
The picture on the right shows that for
\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}

the eigenvalues
\lambda^{\pm}_{1}(\mu,\epsilon)
at fixed
|\epsilon|\ll 1
as
\mu
varies. This figure “8 ” depends on
\mathtt{h}
and shrinks to
0
as
\mathtt{h}\to\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}^{+}
. Some higher order formal expansions have been
computed in Creedon-Deconinck
[
18
]
.

The question which remains open is to determine the stability or instability
of the Stokes waves at the critical Whitham-Benjamin depth
\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
and to analyze
in detail the change of stable-vs-unstable behavior of the eigenvalues along this shallow-to-deep water transient.

Some formal answers have been given so far
[
30
,
32
,
45
,
44
]
. Water waves solutions
in the modulational instability ansatz
are formally approximated by an equation for the wave envelope which is,
if
\mathtt{h}<\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
, a defocusing cubic nonlinear Schr̈odinger equation (NLS)
whereas,
if
\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
, it is a focusing cubic NLS (this is in agreement with the
rigorous stability/instability results in shallow/deep water stated above).
The transient behaviour at the critical depth
\mathtt{h}=\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
corresponds to the vanishing of the cubic coefficients, and, in this case, it is required to determine a
higher order effective NLS.
In the seventies, formal computations by Johnson
[
31
]

suggested the stability of the Stokes waves for nearby
larger values of
\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
.
Some years later Kakutani-Michihiro
[
32
]
derived a different quintic NLS equation, actually claiming the modulational instability of Stokes waves. The instability was further confirmed
by
Slunyaev
[
45
]
who computed how the coefficients of the quintic effective
NLS depend on
\mathtt{h}
.

In this paper we mathematically rigorously prove that
this last scenario is what happens: Stokes waves
of the pure gravity water waves equations at the critical depth are linearly unstable under long wave perturbations.
Unlike all the previous mentioned works, we do not use any formal
approximation argument with some quintic NLS equation, but prove directly the existence of unstable
eigenvalues of the linearized water waves equations at the Stokes wave.
Informally speaking the main result
we prove is the following:

Theorem 1.1
.

(Modulational instability of the Stokes wave at

\mathtt{h}=\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
)

If
\mathtt{h}=\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
then
small
amplitude Stokes waves of amplitude
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)

are linearly unstable subject to long wave perturbations.
Actually
Stokes waves are modulational unstable also at nearby depths
\mathtt{h}<\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
:
there is an analytic function defined for
\epsilon
small,
of the form

\underline{\mathtt{h}}(\epsilon)=-c\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\,,\quad c>0\,,

such that, for any
(\mathtt{h},\epsilon)
satisfying

\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}+\underline{\mathtt{h}}(\epsilon)\,,

(1.1)

then the linearized equations
at the Stokes wave have two eigenvalues with nontrivial real part for any
Floquet exponent
\mu
small enough, see Figure
2
.
In particular for
\mathtt{h}=\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
the unstable eigenvalues depict a
closed figure “8” as
\mu
varies
in an interval of size
[0,c_{1}\epsilon^{2})
,
see Figure
3
, whose height and width
are much smaller
than for
\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
, see Figure
1
.

Figure 2
:

The values of (
\mathtt{h},\epsilon
) in
(0,\infty)\times(0,\epsilon_{1})

for which there are Benjamin-Feir unstable eigenvalues fill the zone above the red curve, where
\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};0,\epsilon)>0
.

Figure 3
:

The figure "8" at the critical depth, which has a smaller size with respect to the one in Figure

1
.

For a more rigorous statement we refer to Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
.
Actually Theorems
1.3
and
1.4

provide a necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of unstable
eigenvalues: the Benjamin-Feir discriminant
function
\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)
that appears in the
matrix entry
[\mathtt{U}]_{21}
in (
1.18
) has to be positive.
We prove in Theorem
1.3
that
the Benjamin-Feir discriminant
function
admits the expansion

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon):=8\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})\epsilon^{2}+8\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})\epsilon^{4}+r_{1}(\epsilon^{5},\mu\epsilon^{3})-\mathtt{e}_{22}(\mathtt{h})\mu^{2}\big(1+r_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\epsilon,\mu)\big)

(1.2)

where

\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}):=\frac{1}{{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}}\Big[\frac{9{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-10{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+9}{8{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}}-\frac{1}{\mathtt{h}-\frac{1}{4}\mathtt{e}_{12}^{2}}\Big(1+\frac{1-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}}{2}+\frac{3}{4}\frac{(1-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4})^{2}}{{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}}\mathtt{h}\Big)\Big]

(1.3)

is called the Benjamin-Feir function,
the coefficient
\mathtt{e}_{22}(\mathtt{h})>0
is defined in (
3.4c
), and the coefficient

\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})
is computed in (
1.20
). The graph of the Benjamin-Feir function
\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})
is as in Figure
4
.

Figure 4
:
The Whitham-Benjamin function
\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})

has a unique root
\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}=1.363\dots
.

Thus, for any
\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
, resp.
\mathtt{h}<\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
, it results
\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})>0
,
resp.
\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})<0
,
and therefore for
\mu
and
\epsilon

small enough
\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)>0
, resp.
\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)<0
, proving the existence of
unstable, resp. stable, eigenvalues.
This is the result proved in
[
10
]
.

On the other hand at
\mathtt{h}=\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
the coefficient
\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})=0
vanishes and the sign of the Benjamin-Feir function

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)
in (
1.2
) is determined by the sign of the coefficient

\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})

(note that in (
1.2
) no pure term of order
\epsilon^{3}
appears;
such a degeneracy is a consequence of symmetries of the problem).
The constant
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})
is computed in (
1.20
) and it turns out to be strictly
positive
.

Figure 5
:

The plot of the function
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})
looks positive for every depth.

This proves the linear instability of the Stokes wave at
\mathtt{h}=\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
, stated in Theorem
1.1
.
In addition the regions
where

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};0,\epsilon)>0
, respectively
\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};0,\epsilon)<0
, are delimited by
the graph of an analytic curve of the form (obtained by the analytic implicit function theorem)

\epsilon\mapsto\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}+\underline{\mathtt{h}}(\epsilon)\,,\quad\underline{\mathtt{h}}(\epsilon)=-\frac{\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})}{\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}^{\prime}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})}\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\,,\qquad\text{solving}\quad\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}+\underline{\mathtt{h}}(\epsilon);0,\epsilon)=0\,,

see Figure
2
. It turns out that

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)>0
if the condition (
1.1
) holds.

In order to prove (
1.2
) –which is the
the major achievement of Sections
3
and
4
–
we need to expand the Stokes waves up to order
\epsilon^{4}
, as provided in
Appendix
A
, and to explicitly compute the
Taylor expansion of
[
10
]
at the fourth order of accuracy.
We implement an effective algorithm to compute (
1.2
)
observing several analytical cancellations in the symplectic
Kato reduction process.
We now present rigorously the main results.

Main result.

We first shortly introduce the pure gravity water waves equations,
their Hamiltonian formulation, and the linearized water waves equations at
the Stokes waves. We
refer to
[
10
]
for more details.

The water waves equations.

We consider the Euler equations for a 2-dimensional incompressible, irrotational fluid under the action of gravity. The fluid fills the
region

{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta}:=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;-\mathtt{h}\leq y<\eta(t,x)\right\}\,,\quad\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\,,

with finite depth
and space periodic boundary conditions.
The irrotational velocity field is the gradient
of a harmonic scalar potential
\Phi=\Phi(t,x,y)

determined by its trace
\psi(t,x)=\Phi(t,x,\eta(t,x))
at the free surface

y=\eta(t,x)
.
Actually
\Phi
is the unique solution of the elliptic equation

\Delta\Phi=0
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta}
with Dirichlet datum
\Phi(t,x,\eta(t,x))=\psi(t,x)
and
\Phi_{y}(t,x,y)=0
at

y=-\mathtt{h}
.

The time evolution of the fluid is determined by two boundary conditions at the free surface.
The first is that the fluid particles remain, along the evolution, on the free surface
and the second one is that the pressure of the fluid
is equal, at the free surface, to the constant atmospheric pressure.
Then, as shown by Zakharov
[
52
]
and Craig-Sulem
[
17
]
,
the time evolution of the fluid is determined by the
following equations for the unknowns
(\eta(t,x),\psi(t,x))
,

\eta_{t}=G(\eta)\psi\,,\quad\psi_{t}=-g\eta-\dfrac{\psi_{x}^{2}}{2}+\dfrac{1}{2(1+\eta_{x}^{2})}\big(G(\eta)\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x}\big)^{2}\,,

(1.4)

where
g>0
is the gravity constant and
G(\eta):=G(\eta,\mathtt{h})
denotes
the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
[G(\eta)\psi](x):=\Phi_{y}(x,\eta(x))-\Phi_{x}(x,\eta(x))\eta_{x}(x)
.
In the sequel, with no loss of generality, we set the gravity constant
g=1
.

The equations (
1.4
) are the Hamiltonian system

\partial_{t}\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix}=\mathcal{J}\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{\eta}\mathcal{H}\\
\nabla_{\psi}\mathcal{H}\end{bmatrix},\quad\quad\mathcal{J}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathrm{Id}\\
-\mathrm{Id}&0\end{bmatrix},

(1.5)

where
\nabla
denote the
L^{2}
-gradient, and the Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}(\eta,\psi):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\psi\,G(\eta)\psi+\eta^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x

is the sum of the kinetic and potential energy of the fluid.
In addition of being Hamiltonian, the water waves
system (
1.4
)
is reversible with respect to the involution

\rho\begin{bmatrix}\eta(x)\\
\psi(x)\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}\eta(-x)\\
-\psi(-x)\end{bmatrix},\quad\text{i.e. }\mathcal{H}\circ\rho=\mathcal{H}\,,

(1.6)

and it
is space invariant.

Stokes waves.

The Stokes waves are traveling
solutions of (
1.4
) of
the form
\eta(t,x)=\breve{\eta}(x-ct)
and
\psi(t,x)=\breve{\psi}(x-ct)
for some real
c
and
2\pi
-periodic functions
(\breve{\eta}(x),\breve{\psi}(x))
.
In a reference frame in translational motion with constant speed
c
, the water waves equations (
1.4
) become

\eta_{t}=c\eta_{x}+G(\eta)\psi\,,\quad\psi_{t}=c\psi_{x}-\eta-\dfrac{\psi_{x}^{2}}{2}+\dfrac{1}{2(1+\eta_{x}^{2})}\big(G(\eta)\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x}\big)^{2}

(1.7)

and the Stokes waves
(\breve{\eta},\breve{\psi})
are equilibrium
steady solutions
of (
1.7
).

Small amplitude Stokes waves were constructed by Struik
[
46
]
in finite depth, and Levi-Civita
[
36
]
,
and Nekrasov
[
39
]
in infinite depth.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Stokes waves)
For any
\mathtt{h}\in(0,+\infty]
there exist
\epsilon_{*}=\epsilon_{*}(\mathtt{h})>0
and a unique family of real analytic solutions
(\eta_{\epsilon}(x),\psi_{\epsilon}(x),c_{\epsilon})
, parameterized by the amplitude
|\epsilon|<\epsilon_{*}
, of

c\,\eta_{x}+G(\eta)\psi=0\,,\quad c\,\psi_{x}-\eta-\dfrac{\psi_{x}^{2}}{2}+\dfrac{1}{2(1+\eta_{x}^{2})}\big(G(\eta)\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x}\big)^{2}=0\,,

(1.8)

such that

\eta_{\epsilon}(x)
and
\psi_{\epsilon}(x)
are
2\pi
-periodic;
\eta_{\epsilon}(x)
is even and
\psi_{\epsilon}(x)
is odd, of the form

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\eta_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon\cos(x)+\epsilon^{2}\big(\eta_{2}^{[0]}+\eta_{2}^{[2]}\cos(2x)\big)+\epsilon^{3}\big(\eta_{3}^{[1]}\cos(x)+\eta_{3}^{[3]}\cos(3x)\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\epsilon^{4}\big(\eta_{4}^{[0]}+\eta_{4}^{[2]}\cos(2x)+\eta_{4}^{[4]}\cos(4x)\big)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{5})\,,\end{aligned}

(1.9a)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\psi_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-1}\sin(x)+\epsilon^{2}\psi_{2}^{[2]}\sin(2x)+\epsilon^{3}\big(\psi_{3}^{[1]}\sin(x)+\psi_{3}^{[3]}\sin(3x)\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\epsilon^{4}\big(\psi_{4}^{[2]}\sin(2x)+\psi_{4}^{[4]}\sin(4x)\big)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{5})\,,\end{aligned}

(1.9b)

\displaystyle c_{\epsilon}={\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+\epsilon^{2}c_{2}+\epsilon^{4}c_{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{5})\,,\qquad{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}=\sqrt{\tanh(\mathtt{h})}\,,

(1.9c)

with coefficients given in (
A.1
).

The expansions (
1.9
)
are derived in Proposition
A.1

(they coincide with
[
23
]
after some suitable rescaling, translation and choice of the moving frame, see Remark
A.4
).

Remark.
More general time quasi-periodic traveling Stokes waves
–which are nonlinear superpositions of multiple Stokes waves traveling with rationally independent speeds– have been recently proved
for (
1.4
) in
[
6
]
in finite depth, in
[
22
]
in infinite depth,
and in
[
5
]
for capillary-gravity water waves in any depth.

Linearization at the Stokes waves.

In order to determine the stability/instability of the Stokes waves given by Theorem
1.2
,
we linearize the water waves equations (
1.7
) with
c=c_{\epsilon}
at
(\eta_{\epsilon}(x),\psi_{\epsilon}(x))
.
In
[
10
]

we obtain the autonomous real linear Hamiltonian and reversible system

\!\!\!\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\eta}_{t}\\
\hat{\psi}_{t}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-G(\eta_{\epsilon})B-\partial_{x}\circ(V-c_{\epsilon})&G(\eta_{\epsilon})\\
-1+B(V-c_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}-B\partial_{x}\circ(V-c_{\epsilon})-BG(\eta_{\epsilon})B&-(V-c_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}+BG(\eta_{\epsilon})\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\eta}\\
\hat{\psi}\end{bmatrix}

(1.10)

where
the functions
(V(x),B(x))
are the horizontal and vertical components of the velocity field

(\Phi_{x},\Phi_{y})
at the free surface. The real system (
1.10
) is Hamiltonian and reversible,
i.e. of the form
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}

with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\top}
,
where
\mathcal{A}^{\top}
is the transposed operator with respect the scalar product of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{A}

anti-commutes with the involution
\rho
in (
1.6
).

The linear system (
1.10
) assumes a simpler form by performing
the time-independent symplectic and reversibility preserving
“good unknown of Alinhac”
and “Levi-Civita" conformal change of variables.
As proved in
[
10
,
2
]
there exists a diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{T}
,

x\mapsto x+\mathfrak{p}(x)
, with a small
2\pi
-periodic odd function
\mathfrak{p}(x)
,
such that, defining the associated composition operator
(\mathfrak{P}u)(x):=u(x+\mathfrak{p}(x))
,
system (
1.10
) is conjugated
under the change of variable

h=\mathcal{P}Z^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\eta}\\
\hat{\psi}\end{bmatrix}\,,\quad\mathcal{P}:=\begin{bmatrix}(1+\mathfrak{p}_{x})\mathfrak{P}&0\\
0&\mathfrak{P}\end{bmatrix}\circ\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
-B&1\end{bmatrix}\,,

(1.11)

into
the linear system
h_{t}=\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}h

where
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
is the Hamiltonian and reversible real operator

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}:=\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{x}\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D|\tanh((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D|)\\
-(1+a_{\epsilon}(x))&({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))\partial_{x}\end{bmatrix}=\mathcal{J}\begin{bmatrix}1+a_{\epsilon}(x)&-({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{x}\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D|\tanh((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D|)\end{bmatrix}

(1.12)

where
p_{\epsilon}(x)
,
a_{\epsilon}(x)
are even real functions
and
\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon}
is small real constant.
The functions

p_{\epsilon}
and
a_{\epsilon}
are analytic in
\epsilon
as maps
B(\epsilon_{0})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{T})

and admit a Taylor expansion as in Proposition
A.7
.
The function
\epsilon\mapsto\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon}
is analytic as well
with a Taylor expansion as in (
A.47
)-(
A.48
).

Bloch-Floquet operator.

Since the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
in (
1.12
) has
2\pi
-periodic coefficients, Bloch-Floquet theory guarantees that the spectrum

\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon})=\bigcup_{\mu\in[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})}\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}(\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon})\qquad\text{where}\quad\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:=e^{-\mathrm{i}\,\mu x}\,\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\,\mu x}\,,

and, if
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})

with eigenvector
v(x)
, then
h(t,x)=e^{\lambda t}e^{\mathrm{i}\,\mu x}v(x)

is a solution of
h_{t}=\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}h
.

The Floquet operator associated with the real operator
\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}
in (
1.12
)
turns out to be the complex
Hamiltonian
and
reversible
pseudo-differential
operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D+\mu|\tanh\big((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D+\mu|\big)\\
-(1+a_{\epsilon}(x))&({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\end{bmatrix}

(1.13)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathrm{Id}\\
-\mathrm{Id}&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\displaystyle{=\mathcal{J}}}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}1+a_{\epsilon}(x)&-({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\\
(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D+\mu|\tanh\big((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D+\mu|\big)\end{bmatrix}}_{\displaystyle{=:\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}}}\,,

meaning that

\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}^{*}
and

\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\circ\overline{\rho}=-\overline{\rho}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
,
where
\overline{\rho}
is the complex involution (cfr. (
1.6
))

\overline{\rho}\begin{bmatrix}\eta(x)\\
\psi(x)\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\eta}(-x)\\
-\overline{\psi}(-x)\end{bmatrix}\,.

Equivalently the self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
is
reversibility-preserving
, i.e.

\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\circ\overline{\rho}=\overline{\rho}\circ\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
.

We regard
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
as an operator with
domain
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}):=H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})

and range
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}):=L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, equipped with
the complex scalar product

(f,g):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(f_{1}\overline{g_{1}}+f_{2}\overline{g_{2}}\right)\,\text{d}x\,,\quad\forall f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{bmatrix},\ \ g=\begin{bmatrix}g_{1}\\
g_{2}\end{bmatrix}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\,.

(1.14)

We also denote
\|f\|^{2}=(f,f)
.

In addition
(\mu,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\in\mathcal{L}(H^{1}(\mathbb{T}),L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
is analytic,
since the functions
\epsilon\mapsto a_{\epsilon}
,
p_{\epsilon}
defined in (
A.58
) are analytic as maps
B(\epsilon_{0})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{T})

and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
is analytic with respect to
\mu
.

Remark.
The spectrum

\sigma({\mathcal{L}}_{-\mu,\epsilon})=\overline{\sigma({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon})}
and
we can restrict to
\mu>0
.
Furthermore
\sigma({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon})
is a 1-periodic set with respect to
\mu
, so one can restrict to
\mu\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
.

Krein criterion
.
In view of the Hamiltonian structure of
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
,
eigenvalues with non zero real part may arise only from multiple
eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,0}

because if
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
then also
-\overline{\lambda}
is, and the total algebraic multiplicity of the eigenvalues is conserved under small perturbation.
The Fourier multiplier matrix real operator

\mathcal{L}_{0,0}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\partial_{x}&|D|\,\tanh\big(\mathtt{h}|D|\big)\\
-1&{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\partial_{x}\end{bmatrix}

possesses the eigenvalue
0
with algebraic multiplicity
4
,
and geometric multiplicity
3
.
A real basis of the corresponding generalized eigenspace is

f_{1}^{+}:=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{1/2}\cos(x)\\
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-1/2}\sin(x)\end{bmatrix},\quad f_{1}^{-}:=\begin{bmatrix}-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{1/2}\sin(x)\\
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-1/2}\cos(x)\end{bmatrix},\quad f_{0}^{+}:=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix},\quad f_{0}^{-}:=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix},

(1.15)

where
f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{-},f_{0}^{-}
are eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}_{0,0}
and

f_{0}^{+}
is a generalized eigenvector, namely
\mathcal{L}_{0,0}f_{0}^{+}=-f_{0}^{-}
.
Furthermore
0
is an isolated eigenvalue for
\mathcal{L}_{0,0}
, namely the
spectrum
\sigma\left(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}\right)
decomposes in two separated parts

\sigma\left(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}\right)=\sigma^{\prime}\left(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}\right)\cup\sigma^{\prime\prime}\left(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}\right)\quad\text{where}\quad\sigma^{\prime}(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}):=\{0\}

(1.16)

and

\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{L}_{0,0})
is formed by non zero eigenvalues at a positive distance from
0
.
In this paper we study the spectrum of
{\cal L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
near
0
(an interesting
problem concerns the splitting of the other non zero eigenvalues, see
[
19
]
).
By Kato’s perturbation theory (see Lemma
2.1
below)
for any
\mu,\epsilon\neq 0
sufficiently small, the perturbed spectrum

\sigma\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)
admits a disjoint decomposition as

\sigma\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)=\sigma^{\prime}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)\cup\sigma^{\prime\prime}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)

where
\sigma^{\prime}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)
consists of 4 eigenvalues close to 0.
We denote by
\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}
the spectral subspace associated with
\sigma^{\prime}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)
, which has dimension 4 and it is invariant by
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}\to\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}
.
The next Theorem
1.3
provides
the complete splitting
of the eigenvalues of the
4\times 4
matrix
which represents
the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}\to\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}
.
Before stating it, we first introduce a notation used through all the paper:

Notation:

we denote by
\mathcal{O}(\mu^{m_{1}}\epsilon^{n_{1}},\dots,\mu^{m_{p}}\epsilon^{n_{p}})
,
m_{j},n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
(for us
\mathbb{N}:=\{1,2,\dots\}
),
analytic functions of
(\mu,\epsilon)
with values in a Banach space
X
which satisfy, for some
C>0
uniform for
\mathtt{h}
in any compact set of
(0,+\infty)
, the bound

\|\mathcal{O}(\mu^{m_{j}}\epsilon^{n_{j}})\|_{X}\leq C\sum_{j=1}^{p}|\mu|^{m_{j}}|\epsilon|^{n_{j}}

for small values of
(\mu,\epsilon)
.
Similarly we denote
r_{k}(\mu^{m_{1}}\epsilon^{n_{1}},\dots,\mu^{m_{p}}\epsilon^{n_{p}})

scalar functions
\mathcal{O}(\mu^{m_{1}}\epsilon^{n_{1}},\dots,\mu^{m_{p}}\epsilon^{n_{p}})
which are also
real
analytic.

Our complete spectral result is the following:

Theorem 1.3
.

(Complete Benjamin-Feir spectrum)

There exist
\epsilon_{0},\mu_{0}>0
, uniformly for the
depth
\mathtt{h}
in any compact set of
(0,+\infty)
,
such that,
for any
0\,<\,\mu<\mu_{0}
and
0\leq\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
,
the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}\to\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}

can be represented by a
4\times 4
matrix of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\mathtt{U}&0\\
0&\mathtt{S}\end{pmatrix},

(1.17)

where
\mathtt{U}
and
\mathtt{S}
are
2\times 2

matrices, with identical purely imaginary diagonal entries each, of the form

\displaystyle\mathtt{U}={\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}\,\big(({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathtt{e}_{12})\mu+r_{2}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})\big)&-\mathtt{e}_{22}\frac{\mu}{8}(1+r_{5}(\epsilon,\mu))\\
-\frac{\mu}{8}\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)&\mathrm{i}\,\big(({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathtt{e}_{12})\mu+r_{2}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})\big)\end{pmatrix}}\,,

(1.18)

\displaystyle\mathtt{S}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}\,{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mu+\mathrm{i}\,{r_{9}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon)}&\tanh(\mathtt{h}\mu)+{r_{10}(\mu\epsilon)}\\
-\mu+{r_{8}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{3}\epsilon)}&\mathrm{i}\,{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mu+\mathrm{i}\,{r_{9}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon)}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(1.19)

The Benjamin-Feir discriminant function
\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)
in (
1.18
)
has the form (
1.2
),
where
\mathtt{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})
is the Whitham-Benjamin function
in (
1.3
), the coefficient

\mathtt{e}_{22}(\mathtt{h})>0
is in (
3.4c
), and

\displaystyle\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})

\displaystyle:={\scriptsize\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{256}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-19}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+1)^{-1}\big({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-2({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+1)\mathtt{h}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-1)^{2}\mathtt{h}^{2}\big)^{-4}\end{matrix}}\cdot

(1.20)

\displaystyle\cdot{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}\Big[\big(476{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}+532{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}-3973{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}-4361{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}+17173{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}+17557{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-37778{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-37754{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-8898{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}-8442{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}+855{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}+963{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+81{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+81\big){\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-8\big(432{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{30}+480{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{28}-3057{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-3361{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}+11452{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}+11544{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}-25989{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}-25749{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}+3928{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}+4384{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}-555{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-171{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}+396{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}+504{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+81{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+81\big)\mathtt{h}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}+4\big(2612{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{34}+2876{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{32}-15531{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{30}-17239{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{28}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}+44053{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}+44277{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}-82191{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}-81283{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}+5921{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}+9353{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}+6831{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}+10203{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+23007{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}+24975{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-117{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}+639{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+567{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+567\big)\mathtt{h}^{2}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-8\big(2128{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{38}+2304{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{36}-11055{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{34}-12463{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{32}+19370{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{30}+20126{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{28}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-5794{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}-5594{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}-51646{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}-49154{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}+57448{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}+59416{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-28802{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-26582{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+32754{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}+33786{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-2682{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}-1926{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+567{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+567\big)\mathtt{h}^{3}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}+2\big(8020{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{42}+8380{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{40}-41279{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{38}-46795{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{36}+49331{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{34}+57267{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{32}+86052{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{30}+84516{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{28}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-274180{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}-267924{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}+176654{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}+178806{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}+104434{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}+101746{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-211660{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-205420{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}+181752{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}+181152{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-24615{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}-20835{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+2835{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+2835\big)\mathtt{h}^{4}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-8\big({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-1\big)^{2}\big(1072{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{38}+1024{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{36}-4711{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{34}-5399{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{32}+4546{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{30}+5302{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{28}-4162{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}-3850{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}+13442{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}+15070{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}-11088{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}-10992{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-9066{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-7806{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+29442{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}+29466{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-5706{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}-4950{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+567{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+567\big){\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}\mathtt{h}^{5}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}+4\left({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-1\right)^{4}\big(564{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{34}+380{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{32}-3755{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{30}-4087{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{28}+8917{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}+9557{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}-15215{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-14499{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}+9953{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}+10313{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-8273{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-7109{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+24159{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}+24111{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-6165{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-5409{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+567{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+567\big)\mathtt{h}^{6}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}\end{matrix}}{\scriptsize\begin{matrix}-8\left({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-1\right)^{6}\big(16{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{30}-32{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{28}-521{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}-489{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}+1252{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}+1344{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-1853{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}-1901{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-512{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-344{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+3333{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}+3285{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-900{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}-792{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+81{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+81\big)\mathtt{h}^{7}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}-\left({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-1\right)^{8}\big(36{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}+108{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}+261{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}+73{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}-1429{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}-1237{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}+3666{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}\end{matrix}}

\displaystyle\scriptsize{\displaystyle\begin{matrix}+3450{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}-3774{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}-3654{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}+873{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}+765{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-81{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}-81\big)\mathtt{h}^{8}\Big]\end{matrix}}\,.

A
numerical calculus performed by Mathematica reveals that

\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})\approx 5.65555>0

(1.21)

and we deduce Theorem
1.1
since
eigenvalues with nonzero real part appear whenever the Benjamin-Feir discriminant

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)>0
. In the
following corollary of Theorem
1.3

we describe the unstable eigenvalues of
{\cal L}_{\mu,\epsilon}

at the critical depth
\mathtt{h}=\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}

(for simplicity we avoid to state the result for
any

(\mathtt{h},\epsilon)
satisfying (
1.1
)).

Theorem 1.4
.

(Benjamin-Feir unstable eigenvalues at

\mathtt{h}=\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}.
\bf)

There exist
\epsilon_{1},\mu_{0}>0
and
an analytic function
\underline{\mu}(\cdot):[0,\epsilon_{1})\to[0,\mu_{0})

of the form

\underline{\mu}(\epsilon)=\underline{c}\,\epsilon^{2}\big(1+r(\epsilon)\big)\,,\quad\underline{c}:=\sqrt{\frac{8\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})}{\mathtt{e}_{22}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})}}\,,

(1.22)

such that,
for any
\epsilon\in[0,\epsilon_{1})
, the
operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
has two eigenvalues

\lambda^{\pm}_{1}(\mu,\epsilon)

\begin{cases}\mathrm{i}\,\frac{1}{2}\breve{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mu+\mathrm{i}\,r_{2}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})\pm\tfrac{1}{8}\mu\sqrt{\mathtt{e}_{22}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})}(1+r(\epsilon,\mu))\sqrt{\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}};\mu,\epsilon)}&0<\mu<\underline{\mu}(\epsilon)\\[4.2679pt]
\mathrm{i}\,\frac{1}{2}\breve{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mu+\mathrm{i}\,r_{2}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})\pm\mathrm{i}\,\tfrac{1}{8}\mu\sqrt{\mathtt{e}_{22}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})}(1+r(\epsilon,\mu))\sqrt{|\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}};\mu,\epsilon)|}&\underline{\mu}(\epsilon)\leq\mu<\mu_{0}\,,\end{cases}\!\!\!

(1.23)

with

\breve{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}:=2{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}-\mathtt{e}_{12}(\mathtt{h})>0

and
\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}};\mu,\epsilon)=8\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})\epsilon^{4}+r_{1}(\epsilon^{5},\mu\epsilon^{3})-\mathtt{e}_{22}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})\mu^{2}\big(1+r_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\epsilon,\mu)\big)
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
. Since

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}};0,\epsilon)=8\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})\epsilon^{4}(1+r(\epsilon))
, it results that

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}};\mu,\epsilon)>0
, for any
\mu\in(0,\underline{\mu}(\epsilon))
as in (
1.22
) and
\epsilon
small enough.
The unstable eigenvalues
\lambda^{\pm}_{1}(\mu,\epsilon)
in (
1.22
)
are those of the matrix
\mathtt{U}
in (
1.18
).
In order to determine
the value
\mu=\underline{\mu}(\epsilon)
such that
\lambda^{\pm}_{1}(\mu,\epsilon)
touches the imaginary axis far from the origin, we set

\mu=c\epsilon^{2}
so that

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}};\mu,\epsilon)=0
if and only if

0=\epsilon^{-4}\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}};c\epsilon^{2},\epsilon)=8\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})(1+r(\epsilon))+r_{1}(c\epsilon)-\mathtt{e}_{22}(\mathtt{h}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}})c^{2}\big(1+r_{1}(\epsilon)\big)\,.

This equation is
solved by an analytic function
\epsilon\mapsto c_{\epsilon}=\underline{c}(1+r(\epsilon))

with
\underline{c}
defined in (
1.22
).
∎

We conclude this section describing the main steps of the proof and
the organization of the paper.

Ideas and scheme of proof.
In Section
2
we shortly report the results of

[
10
]
which reduce the problem to determine the eigenvalues of
the
4\times 4
Hamiltonian and reversible matrix

\mathtt{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathtt{J}\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.8
).
Then in Section
3
we provide the Taylor
expansion of the matrix
\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.9
)
at an order of accuracy higher than
in
[
10
, Proposition 4.3]
.
In particular in Proposition

3.1
we compute the coefficients of the Taylor expansion up to order
4

in
the matrix entries (
3.3a
)-(
3.3c
)
which enter in the constant
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})
(cfr. (
4.9
))
appearing in the Benjamin-Feir discriminant function (
1.2
).
This explicit computation requires the knowledge of the
Taylor expansions of the Kato spectral projectors
P_{\mu,\epsilon}
up to cubic order,
that we provide in Section
3.2
and prove in Appendix
B
, relying on complex analysis. In order to perform effective computations
we observe several analytical cancellations in Sections
3.3
and

3.4
, which reduce considerably the number of explicit scalar products
to compute.
The proof of Proposition

3.1
requires ultimately the
knowledge of the Taylor expansion up to order four
of the Levi-Civita and Alinach good unknown transformations (
1.11
)
and of the functions
a_{\epsilon}(x),p_{\epsilon}(x)
in the operator
{\cal L}_{\mu,\epsilon}

in (
1.13
), which are derived in Appendix
A.2
.
In turn, such expansions follow by those of the Stokes waves
that we prove
in Appendix
A.1
. Finally in Section
4
we
implement the block-diagonalization procedure of
[
10
, Section 5]
which provides the
block-diagonal matrix (
1.18
)
and we analytically compute the expansion of the
Benjamin-Feir discriminant function
\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{BF}}}(\mathtt{h};\mu,\epsilon)
, in particular of the
constant
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})
in (
4.9
) and thus (
1.20
).

We point out that the constant

\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
in (
4.9
) is
analytically
computed in terms of the
coefficients (
4.4
) which, in turn,
are expressed
in terms of the coefficients

\phi_{21},\phi_{22},\gamma_{12},\eta_{12},\gamma_{11},\phi_{11},\gamma_{22},\phi_{12},\mathtt{f}_{11}
, and ultimately

\eta_{2}^{[0]},\ldots,\eta_{4}^{[4]},\psi_{2}^{[2]},\dots,\psi_{4}^{[4]}
,

c_{2},c_{4}

of the Stokes wave provided in Appendix
A.1
. Then we
used Mathematica to compute how the coefficients of the Stokes wave, of the functions
a_{\epsilon}(x)
,
p_{\epsilon}(x)
in (
1.13
), and
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}(\mathtt{h})
in (
1.20
) depend on
\mathtt{h}
, starting from their algebraic formulas.
The Mathematica code employed can be found at

https://git-scm.sissa.it/amaspero/benjamin-feir-instability
.

“Hamiltonian and dispersive PDEs”.

Data availability statement:
the Mathematica code used to perform some computations is available at https://git-scm.sissa.it/amaspero/benjamin-feir-instability.

2
Perturbative approach to separated eigenvalues

In this section we shortly report the spectral procedure developed in
[
10
,
7
]

to study the splitting of the eigenvalues of

\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
close to
0
for small values of
\mu
and
\epsilon
.
First of all we decompose the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
1.13
) as

\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathrm{i}\,{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mu+\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,,\qquad\mu>0\,,

(2.1)

where

\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
is the Hamiltonian and reversible operator

\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathcal{J}\,{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,,\quad{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}:=\begin{bmatrix}1+a_{\epsilon}(x)&-({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))\partial_{x}-\mathrm{i}\,\mu\,p_{\epsilon}(x)\\
\partial_{x}\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))+\mathrm{i}\,\mu\,p_{\epsilon}(x)&|D+\mu|\,\tanh\big((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D+\mu|\big)\end{bmatrix}

(2.2)

with
{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
selfadjoint.
The operator

\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
is analytic with respect to
(\mu,\epsilon)
as
\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
is.
The operator
\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:Y\subset X\to X

has domain
Y:=H^{1}(\mathbb{T}):=H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and range
X:=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}):=L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

In view of (
2.1
), the spectrum

\sigma({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon})=\mathrm{i}\,{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mu+\sigma(\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon})

and we focus on studying the spectrum of
\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
.
The unperturbed
\mathscr{L}_{0,0}=\mathcal{L}_{0,0}
has zero as isolated eigenvalue with algebraic multiplicity 4, geometric multiplicity 3 and generalized kernel spanned by the vectors
\{f^{+}_{1},f^{-}_{1},f^{+}_{0},f^{-}_{0}\}
in (
1.15
).
The following lemma is
[
10
, Lemmata 3.1 and 3.2]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

(Kato theory for separated eigenvalues of Hamiltonian operators)

Let
\Gamma
be a closed, counterclockwise-oriented curve around
0
in the complex plane separating
\sigma^{\prime}\left(\mathscr{L}_{0,0}\right)=\{0\}

and the other part of the spectrum
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\left(\mathscr{L}_{0,0}\right)
in (
1.16
).
There exist
\epsilon_{0},\mu_{0}>0
such that for any
(\mu,\epsilon)\in B(\mu_{0})\times B(\epsilon_{0})
the following statements hold:

1. The curve
\Gamma
belongs to the resolvent set of
the operator
\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:Y\subset X\to X
defined in (
2.2
).

2.
The operators

P_{\mu,\epsilon}:=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\,}\oint_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}-\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda:X\to Y

(2.3)

are well defined projectors commuting with
\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
, i.e.

P_{\mu,\epsilon}^{2}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}
and

P_{\mu,\epsilon}\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{\mu,\epsilon}
.
The map
(\mu,\epsilon)\mapsto P_{\mu,\epsilon}
is analytic from

B({\mu_{0}})\times B({\epsilon_{0}})

to
\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
.
The projectors
P_{\mu,\epsilon}
are
skew-Hamiltonian and reversibility preserving, i.e.

\mathcal{J}P_{\mu,\epsilon}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}^{*}\mathcal{J}\,,\quad\overline{\rho}P_{\mu,\epsilon}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}\overline{\rho}\,.

(2.4)

Finally

P_{0,\epsilon}
is a real operator, i.e.
\overline{P_{0,\epsilon}}=P_{0,\epsilon}
.

3.
The domain
Y
of the operator
\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
decomposes as the direct sum

Y=\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}\oplus\text{Ker}(P_{\mu,\epsilon})\,,\quad\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}:=\text{Rg}(P_{\mu,\epsilon})=\text{Ker}(\mathrm{Id}-P_{\mu,\epsilon})\,,

of closed invariant subspaces, namely

\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}\to\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}
,
\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:\text{Ker}(P_{\mu,\epsilon})\to\text{Ker}(P_{\mu,\epsilon})
.
Moreover

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon})\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mbox{ inside }\Gamma\}=\sigma(\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}|_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\mu,\epsilon}})=\sigma^{\prime}(\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}),

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon})\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mbox{ outside }\Gamma\}=\sigma(\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}|_{Ker(P_{\mu,\epsilon})})=\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon})\,.

4. The projectors
P_{\mu,\epsilon}

are similar one to each other: the transformation operators

U_{\mu,\epsilon}:=\big(\mathrm{Id}-(P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0,0})^{2}\big)^{-1/2}\big[P_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{0,0}+(\mathrm{Id}-P_{\mu,\epsilon})(\mathrm{Id}-P_{0,0})\big]

(2.5)

are bounded and invertible in
Y
and in
X
, with inverse

U_{\mu,\epsilon}^{-1}=\big[P_{0,0}P_{\mu,\epsilon}+(\mathrm{Id}-P_{0,0})(\mathrm{Id}-P_{\mu,\epsilon})\big]\big(\mathrm{Id}-(P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0,0})^{2}\big)^{-1/2}\,,

and

U_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{0,0}U_{\mu,\epsilon}^{-1}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}
as well as
U_{\mu,\epsilon}^{-1}P_{\mu,\epsilon}U_{\mu,\epsilon}=P_{0,0}
.
The map
(\mu,\epsilon)\mapsto U_{\mu,\epsilon}
is analytic from
B(\mu_{0})\times B(\epsilon_{0})
to
\mathcal{L}(Y)
.
The transformation operators
U_{\mu,\epsilon}
are symplectic and reversibility preserving, namely

U_{\mu,\epsilon}^{*}\mathcal{J}U_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathcal{J}\,,\qquad\overline{\rho}U_{\mu,\epsilon}=U_{\mu,\epsilon}\overline{\rho}\,.

(2.6)

Finally
U_{0,\epsilon}
is a real operator, i.e.
\overline{U_{0,\epsilon}}=U_{0,\epsilon}
.

5. The subspaces
\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\text{Rg}(P_{\mu,\epsilon})
are isomorphic one to each other:

\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}=U_{\mu,\epsilon}\mathcal{V}_{0,0}.

In particular
\dim\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\dim\mathcal{V}_{0,0}=4
, for any

(\mu,\epsilon)\in B(\mu_{0})\times B(\epsilon_{0})
.

We consider the basis of the subspace
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathrm{Rg}(P_{\mu,\epsilon})
,

\mathcal{F}:=\big\{f_{1}^{+}(\mu,\epsilon),\ f_{1}^{-}(\mu,\epsilon),\ f_{0}^{+}(\mu,\epsilon),\ f_{0}^{-}(\mu,\epsilon)\big\}\,,\quad f_{k}^{\sigma}(\mu,\epsilon):=U_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{k}^{\sigma}\,,\ \sigma=\pm\,,\,k=0,1\,,

(2.7)

obtained by applying the transformation operators
U_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.5
)
to the eigenvectors
f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{-},f_{0}^{+},f_{0}^{-}
in (
1.15
)
which form a basis of

\mathcal{V}_{0,0}=\mathrm{Rg}(P_{0,0})
.
Exploiting the property (
2.6
) that the transformation operators
U_{\mu,\epsilon}

are symplectic and reversibility preserving, it is proved in
[
10
, Section 3]

the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

(Matrix representation of
{\cal L}_{\mu,\epsilon}
on
\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}
)

The operator
\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}:\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}\to\mathcal{V}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in
(
2.2
) defined for
(\mu,\epsilon)\in B_{\mu_{0}}(0)\times B_{\epsilon_{0}}(0)

is represented on the basis
\mathcal{F}
in (
2.7
) by the
4\times 4
Hamiltonian and reversible matrix

\mathtt{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathtt{J}\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\quad\text{where}\quad\mathtt{J}:=\mathtt{J}_{4}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathtt{J}_{2}&0\\
0&\mathtt{J}_{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad{\small\mathtt{J}_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}}\,,

(2.8)

and

\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}^{*}

is the
4\times 4
self-adjoint matrix

\displaystyle\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{-},f_{1}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{+},f_{1}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{-},f_{1}^{+}\big)\\
\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{-},f_{1}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{+},f_{1}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{-},f_{1}^{-}\big)\\
\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{+},f_{0}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{-},f_{0}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{+},f_{0}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{-},f_{0}^{+}\big)\\
\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{+},f_{0}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{-},f_{0}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{+},f_{0}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{-},f_{0}^{-}\big)\\
\end{pmatrix}

(2.9)

where

\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}:=P_{0,0}^{*}\,U_{\mu,\epsilon}^{*}\,{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,U_{\mu,\epsilon}\,P_{0,0}\,.

(2.10)

The entries of the matrix
\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}

are alternatively real or purely imaginary: for any
\sigma=\pm
,
k=0,1
,
the scalar product
\left(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,f^{\sigma}_{k}\,,\,f^{\sigma}_{k^{\prime}}\right)
is real
and
\left(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,f^{\sigma}_{k}\,,\,f^{-\sigma}_{k^{\prime}}\right)

is purely imaginary.
The matrix
\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
is analytic in
(\mu,\epsilon)
close to
(0,0)
.

We conclude this section recalling some notation.
A
2n\times 2n
,
n=1,2,
matrix of the form

\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{J}_{2n}\mathtt{B}
is
Hamiltonian
if
\mathtt{B}
is a self-adjoint matrix, i.e.
\mathtt{B}=\mathtt{B}^{*}
.
It is
reversible
if
\mathtt{B}
is reversibility-preserving, i.e.
\rho_{2n}\circ\mathtt{B}=\mathtt{B}\circ\rho_{2n}
, where

\rho_{4}:=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{2}&0\\
0&\rho_{2}\end{pmatrix}
,

\rho_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{c}&0\\
0&-\mathfrak{c}\end{pmatrix}

and
\mathfrak{c}:z\mapsto\overline{z}
is the conjugation of the complex plane.
Equivalently,
\rho_{2n}\circ\mathtt{L}=-\mathtt{L}\circ\rho_{2n}
.

The transformations
preserving the Hamiltonian structure are called

symplectic
, and satisfy

Y^{*}\mathtt{J}_{4}Y=\mathtt{J}_{4}
.
If
Y
is symplectic then
Y^{*}
and
Y^{-1}
are symplectic as well. A Hamiltonian matrix
\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{J}_{4}\mathtt{B}
, with
\mathtt{B}=\mathtt{B}^{*}
, is conjugated through a symplectic matrix

Y
in a new Hamiltonian matrix.
We finally mention
that the flow of a Hamiltonian
reversibility-preserving matrix is symplectic
and reversibility-preserving (see Lemma 3.8 in
[
7
]
).

3
Expansion of
\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}

In this section we provide the Taylor
expansion of the matrix
\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.9
), i.e.
(
3.1
), at an order
of accuracy higher than
in
[
10
, Proposition 4.3]
.
In particular we compute the
quadratic terms
\gamma_{11}\epsilon^{2}
,
\phi_{21}\mu\epsilon
, the cubic ones

\eta_{12}\mu\epsilon^{2}
,
\gamma_{12}\mu\epsilon^{2}
,
\phi_{11}\epsilon^{3}
,
\phi_{22}\mu^{2}\epsilon
,
and the quartic terms
\eta_{11}\epsilon^{4}
,

\gamma_{22}\mu^{2}\epsilon^{2}
,
\phi_{12}\mu\epsilon^{3}
in
the matrices (
3.3a
)-(
3.3c
) below. These are the coefficients
which enter in the constant
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsc{WB}}}
(cfr. (
4.9
))
of the Benjamin-Feir discriminant function (
1.2
).

For convenience we decompose the
4\times 4
matrix
\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}

in (
2.9
) as

\displaystyle\mathtt{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}E(\mu,\epsilon)&F(\mu,\epsilon)\\
F^{*}(\mu,\epsilon)&G(\mu,\epsilon)\end{pmatrix}

(3.1)

where
E
,
G
are the
2\times 2
self-adjoint matrices

\displaystyle E(\mu,\epsilon):=\begin{pmatrix}E_{11}(\mu,\epsilon)&\mathrm{i}\,E_{12}(\mu,\epsilon)\\
-\mathrm{i}\,E_{12}(\mu,\epsilon)&E_{22}(\mu,\epsilon)\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{-},f_{1}^{+}\big)\\
\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{1}^{-},f_{1}^{-}\big)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.2a)

\displaystyle G(\mu,\epsilon):=\begin{pmatrix}G_{11}(\mu,\epsilon)&\mathrm{i}\,G_{12}(\mu,\epsilon)\\
-\mathrm{i}\,G_{12}(\mu,\epsilon)&G_{22}(\mu,\epsilon)\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{+},f_{0}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{-},f_{0}^{+}\big)\\
\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{+},f_{0}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{-},f_{0}^{-}\big)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.2b)

and

\displaystyle F(\mu,\epsilon):=\begin{pmatrix}F_{11}(\mu,\epsilon)&\mathrm{i}\,F_{12}(\mu,\epsilon)\\
\mathrm{i}\,F_{21}(\mu,\epsilon)&F_{22}(\mu,\epsilon)\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{+},f_{1}^{+}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{-},f_{1}^{+}\big)\\
\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{+},f_{1}^{-}\big)&\big(\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}f_{0}^{-},f_{1}^{-}\big)\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.2c)

The main result of this section is the following proposition.

Proposition 3.1
.

The
2\times 2
matrices
E:=E(\mu,\epsilon)
,
F:=F(\mu,\epsilon)
,
G:=G(\mu,\epsilon)
defined in (
3.2
) admit the expansion

\displaystyle E=\begin{pmatrix}\mathtt{e}_{11}\epsilon^{2}(1+r_{1}^{\prime}(\epsilon^{3},\mu\epsilon))+\eta_{11}\epsilon^{4}-\mathtt{e}_{22}\frac{\mu^{2}}{8}(1+r_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\epsilon,\mu))&\mathrm{i}\,\big(\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{e}_{12}\mu{+\eta_{12}\mu\epsilon^{2}}+r_{2}(\mu\epsilon^{3},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})\big)\\
-\mathrm{i}\,\big(\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{e}_{12}\mu{+\eta_{12}\mu\epsilon^{2}}+r_{2}(\mu\epsilon^{3},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})\big)&-\mathtt{e}_{22}\frac{\mu^{2}}{8}(1+r_{5}(\epsilon^{{2}},\mu))\end{pmatrix}

(3.3a)

\displaystyle G=\begin{pmatrix}1{+\gamma_{11}\epsilon^{2}}+r_{8}(\epsilon^{3},\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon)&{-\mathrm{i}\,\gamma_{12}\mu\epsilon^{2}}-\mathrm{i}\,r_{9}(\mu\epsilon^{3},\mu^{2}\epsilon)\\
{\mathrm{i}\,\gamma_{12}\mu\epsilon^{2}}+\mathrm{i}\,r_{9}(\mu\epsilon^{3},\mu^{2}\epsilon)&\mu\tanh(\mathtt{h}\mu){+\gamma_{22}\mu^{2}\epsilon^{2}}+r_{10}(\mu^{2}\epsilon^{3},\mu^{3}\epsilon)\end{pmatrix}

(3.3b)

\displaystyle F=\begin{pmatrix}\mathtt{f}_{11}\epsilon{+\phi_{11}\epsilon^{3}}+r_{3}(\epsilon^{4},\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon)&\mathrm{i}\,\mu\epsilon{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}{+\mathrm{i}\,\phi_{12}\mu\epsilon^{3}}+\mathrm{i}\,r_{4}({\mu\epsilon^{4}},\mu^{2}\epsilon^{2},\mu^{3}\epsilon)\\
\mathrm{i}\,{\phi_{21}\mu\epsilon}+\mathrm{i}\,r_{6}(\mu\epsilon^{{3}},\mu^{2}\epsilon)&{\phi_{22}\mu^{2}\epsilon}+r_{7}(\mu^{2}\epsilon^{{3}},\mu^{3}\epsilon)\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.3c)

where the coefficients

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}_{11}:=\dfrac{9{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-10{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+9}{8{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{7}}=\dfrac{9(1-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4})^{2}+8{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}}{8{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{7}}>0\,,\qquad\mathtt{f}_{11}:=\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4})\,,

(3.4a)

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}_{12}:={\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-1}(1-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4})\mathtt{h}>0\,,

(3.4b)

\displaystyle\mathtt{e}_{22}:=\dfrac{(1-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4})(1+3{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4})\mathtt{h}^{2}+2{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-1)\mathtt{h}+{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}}{{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{3}}>0\,,

(3.4c)

were computed in
[
10
, Proposition 4.3]
, whereas

\displaystyle\eta_{11}:=\frac{1}{256{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{19}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+1)}\big(-36{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{26}-108{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{24}-261{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{22}-73{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}+1429{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}+1237{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}

(3.4d)

\displaystyle\qquad-3666{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-3450{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+3774{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}+3654{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-873{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}-765{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+81{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+81\big)\,,

\displaystyle\eta_{12}:=\frac{{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}\big(3{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}-8{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}+3{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+18\big)-\big({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-2{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+12{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-38{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+27\big)\mathtt{h}}{16{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{9}}\,,

(3.4e)

\displaystyle\gamma_{11}:=\frac{-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}+6{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-5}{8{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}}\,,\quad\gamma_{12}:=\frac{2{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-9}{16{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{7}}\,,\quad\gamma_{22}:=\frac{{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-5}{4{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}}\,,

(3.4f)

\displaystyle\phi_{11}:=\frac{10{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{20}+4{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}-7{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-6{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-99{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+257{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-6{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}-171{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+18}{64{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{27/2}}\,,

(3.4g)

\displaystyle\phi_{12}:=\frac{2{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{18}-2{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{16}-33{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{14}-27{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{12}+34{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{10}+34{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}-33{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{6}-27{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}+18{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+18}{32{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{25/2}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+1)}

(3.4h)

\displaystyle\phi_{21}:=\frac{{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-5)-({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{8}+2{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}-3)\mathtt{h}}{8{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{7/2}}\,,\quad\phi_{22}:=\frac{-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{4}\mathtt{h}+{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}+\mathtt{h}}{4{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{5/2}}\,.

(3.4i)

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of this proposition.

In
[
10
, Proposition 4.3]
we showed that
the matrices
E,G,F
in (
3.3a
), (
3.3b
), (
3.3c
) admit the following expansions

\displaystyle E(\mu,\epsilon)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathtt{e}_{11}\epsilon^{2}-\mathtt{e}_{22}\frac{\mu^{2}}{8}&\mathrm{i}\,\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{e}_{12}\mu\\
-\mathrm{i}\,\frac{1}{2}\mathtt{e}_{12}\mu&-\mathtt{e}_{22}\frac{\mu^{2}}{8}\end{pmatrix}+\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}r_{1}(\epsilon^{3},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})&\mathrm{i}\,r_{2}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})\\
-\mathrm{i}\,r_{2}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})&r_{5}(\mu^{2}\epsilon,\mu^{3})\end{pmatrix}}_{=:{\mathcal{E}}(\mu,\epsilon)}\,,

(3.5a)

\displaystyle G(\mu,\epsilon)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\mu\tanh(\mathtt{h}\mu)\end{pmatrix}+\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}r_{8}(\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon)&-\mathrm{i}\,r_{9}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon)\\
\mathrm{i}\,r_{9}(\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon)&r_{10}(\mu^{2}\epsilon)\,,\end{pmatrix}}_{=:\Gamma(\mu,\epsilon)}

(3.5b)

\displaystyle F(\mu,\epsilon):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathtt{f}_{11}\epsilon&\mathrm{i}\,\mu\epsilon{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}+\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}r_{3}(\epsilon^{3},\mu\epsilon^{2},\mu^{2}\epsilon)&\mathrm{i}\,r_{4}({\mu\epsilon^{2}},\mu^{2}\epsilon)\\
\mathrm{i}\,r_{6}(\mu\epsilon)&r_{7}(\mu^{2}\epsilon)\end{pmatrix}}_{=:\Phi(\mu,\epsilon)}\,.

(3.5c)

In order to get the expansion of

{\mathcal{E}}(\mu,\epsilon)
,
\Gamma(\mu,\epsilon)
and
\Phi(\mu,\epsilon)

in Proposition
3.1
we first
expand the operators
{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.2
) (Section
3.1
),
the projector
P_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.3
) (Section
3.2
)
and the operator
\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.10
) (Section
3.3
).
Finally we prove Proposition
3.1
in Section
3.4
.

Notation.

For an operator
A=A(\mu,\epsilon)
we denote its Taylor coefficients as

A_{i,j}:=\frac{1}{i!j!}\big(\partial^{i}_{\mu}\partial^{j}_{\epsilon}A\big)(0,0)\,,\qquad A_{k}:=A_{k}(\mu,\epsilon):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\\
i,j\geq 0\end{subarray}}A_{i,j}\mu^{i}\epsilon^{j}\,.

(3.6)

Moreover we shall occasionally split
A_{i,j}=A_{i,j}^{[\mathtt{ev}]}+A_{i,j}^{[\mathtt{odd}]}
, where
A_{i,j}^{[\mathtt{ev}]}
is the part of the operator
A_{i,j}
having only even harmonics, whereas
A_{i,j}^{[\mathtt{odd}]}
is the part having only odd ones.

3.1
Expansion of
{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}

In the sequel

\mathcal{O}_{5}
means an operator which maps
H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
into

L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
-functions with size
\epsilon^{5}
,
\mu\epsilon^{4}
,
\mu^{2}\epsilon^{3}
,
\mu^{3}\epsilon^{2}
,
\mu^{4}\epsilon
or
\mu^{5}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The operator
{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.2
) has the Taylor expansion

{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}={\cal B}_{0}+{\cal B}_{1}+{\cal B}_{2}+{\cal B}_{3}+{\cal B}_{4}+\mathcal{O}_{5}\,,

(3.7)

where

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1&-{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\partial_{x}\\
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\partial_{x}&|D|\tanh\big(\mathtt{h}|D|\big)\end{bmatrix}\,,

(3.8a)

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{1}

\displaystyle=\epsilon

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}(x)&-p_{1}(x)\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{x}\circ p_{1}(x)&0\end{bmatrix}+\mu\ell_{1,0}(|D|)\Pi_{\mathtt{s}}\,,

(3.8b)

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{2}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a_{2}(x)&-p_{2}(x)\partial_{x}\\

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{3}

(3.8d)

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{4}

(3.8e)

and

(3.9)

\displaystyle\ell_{1,0}(|D|)

(3.10e)

Proof.

We observe that, using the notation introduced in (

{\cal B}_{i,j}^{[\mathtt{ev}]}=\begin{cases}{\cal B}_{i,j}&\textup{if }j\textup{ is even}\,,\\
0&\textup{if }j\textup{ is odd}\,,\end{cases}\qquad{\cal B}_{i,j}^{[\mathtt{odd}]}=\begin{cases}0&\textup{if }j\textup{ is even}\,,\\
{\cal B}_{i,j}&\textup{if }j\textup{ is odd}\,.\end{cases}

(3.11)

3.2
Expansion of the projector
P_{\mu,\epsilon}

The projectors

P_{\mu,\epsilon}=P_{0}+P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3}+\mathcal{O}_{4}\,,

(3.12)

\displaystyle P_{0}:=P_{0,0}\,,\quad P_{1}:=\mathcal{P}\big[{\cal B}_{1}\big]

(3.13)

\displaystyle P_{2}:=\mathcal{P}\big[{\cal B}_{2}\big]+\mathcal{P}\big[{\cal B}_{1},{\cal B}_{1}\big]\,,

\displaystyle P_{3}:=\mathcal{P}\big[{\cal B}_{3}\big]+\mathcal{P}\big[{\cal B}_{2},{\cal B}_{1}\big]+\mathcal{P}\big[{\cal B}_{1},{\cal B}_{2}\big]+\mathcal{P}\big[{\cal B}_{1},{\cal B}_{1},{\cal B}_{1}\big]\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\big[A_{1}]:=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\,}\oint_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}-\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{J}A_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}-\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda\,,\quad\text{and for }k\geq 2

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\big[A_{1},\dots,A_{k}\big]:=\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\,}\oint_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}-\lambda)^{-1}\mathcal{J}A_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}-\lambda)^{-1}\dots\mathcal{J}A_{k}(\mathcal{L}_{0,0}-\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda\,.

In virtue of (

)-(

P_{i,j}^{[\mathtt{ev}]}=\begin{cases}P_{i,j}&\textup{if }j\textup{ is even}\,,\\
0&\textup{if }j\textup{ is odd}\,,\end{cases}\qquad P_{i,j}^{[\mathtt{odd}]}=\begin{cases}0&\textup{if }j\textup{ is even}\,,\\
P_{i,j}&\textup{if }j\textup{ is odd}\,.\end{cases}

3.3
Expansion of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}

In this section we provide the expansion of the operator
\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
defined in (
2.10
).
We introduce the notation
\textup{{Sym}}[A]:=\frac{1}{2}A+\frac{1}{2}A^{*}
.

Lemma 3.6

(
Expansion of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
)
.

The operator
\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.10
) has the Taylor expansion

\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\sum_{j=0}^{4}\mathfrak{B}_{j}+\mathcal{O}_{5}

(3.23)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{0}

\displaystyle:=P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{0},\quad\mathfrak{B}_{1}:=P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{1}P_{0},\quad\mathfrak{B}_{2}:=P_{0}^{*}\mathbf{Sym}\big[{\cal B}_{2}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{1}\big]P_{0}\,,

(3.24a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{3}

\displaystyle:=P_{0}^{*}\mathbf{Sym}\big[{\cal B}_{3}+{\cal B}_{2}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{1}(\mathrm{Id}-P_{0})P_{2}\big]P_{0}\,,

(3.24b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{4}

\displaystyle:=P_{0}^{*}\mathbf{Sym}\big[{\cal B}_{4}+{\cal B}_{3}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{2}(\mathrm{Id}-P_{0})P_{2}+{\cal B}_{1}(\mathrm{Id}-P_{0})P_{3}-{\cal B}_{1}P_{1}P_{0}P_{2}+\mathfrak{N}P_{0}P_{2}\big]P_{0}\,,

(3.24c)

the operators
P_{0},\ldots,P_{3}
are defined in (
3.13
) and

\mathfrak{N}:=\frac{1}{4}\big(P_{2}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}-{\cal B}_{0}P_{2}\big)=-\mathfrak{N}^{*}\,.

(3.25)

It results

\big(\mathfrak{N}f_{k}^{\sigma},f_{k^{\prime}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\big)=0\,,\quad\forall f_{k}^{\sigma},f_{k^{\prime}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\in\{f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{-},f_{0}^{-}\}\,.

(3.26)

Proof.

In order to expand

\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.10
) we first expand
U_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{0}
.
In view of (
2.5
) we have,
introducing the analytic function

g(x):=(1-x)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for
|x|<1
,

U_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{0}=g((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2})\,P_{\mu,\epsilon}\,P_{0}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}\,g((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2})\,P_{0}\ ,

(3.27)

using that

(P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2}
commutes with
P_{\mu,\epsilon}
, and so does

g((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2})
.
The Taylor expansion
g(x)=1+\frac{1}{2}x+\frac{3}{8}x^{2}+\mathcal{O}(x^{3})
implies that

g((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2})=\mathrm{Id}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}(P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2}}_{=:g_{2}}+\underbrace{\frac{3}{8}(P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{4}}_{=:g_{4}}+\,\mathcal{O}_{6}\,,

(3.28)

where
\mathcal{O}_{6}=\mathcal{O}((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{6})\in\mathcal{L}(Y)
.

Furthermore, since
P_{\mu,\epsilon}\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\mathscr{L}_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{\mu,\epsilon}
(see Lemma
2.1
- item 2),
applying
\mathcal{J}
to both sides and using (
2.4
), yields

P_{\mu,\epsilon}^{*}{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}={\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{\mu,\epsilon}\qquad\text{where}\qquad P_{\mu,\epsilon}^{2}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}\,.

(3.29)

Therefore the operator
\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.10
) has the expansion

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}

\displaystyle\eq^{\eqref{UP}}P_{0}^{*}\,g((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2})^{*}\,P_{\mu,\epsilon}^{*}\,{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,P_{\mu,\epsilon}\,g((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2})\,P_{0}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{PB}}}{{=}}P_{0}^{*}\,g((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2})^{*}\,{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,P_{\mu,\epsilon}\,g((P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2})\,P_{0}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{geppoexp}}}{{=}}P_{0}^{*}\,(\mathrm{Id}+g_{2}^{*}+g_{4}^{*}+\mathcal{O}_{6})\,{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,P_{\mu,\epsilon}\,\big(\mathrm{Id}+g_{2}+g_{4}+\mathcal{O}_{6}\big)\,P_{0}

\displaystyle\;\;\;=\;\;\,\mathbf{Sym}\big[P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{\mu,\epsilon}+2{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{\mu,\epsilon}g_{2}+g_{2}^{*}{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{\mu,\epsilon}g_{2}+2{\cal B}_{\mu,\epsilon}P_{\mu,\epsilon}g_{4}\big)P_{0}\big]\,+\mathcal{O}_{6}

(3.30)

using (
3.29
) and that
g_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{2}
and
g_{4}=\mathcal{O}_{4}
.

A further analysis of the term (
3.30
) relies on the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\Pi_{0}^{+}
be the orthogonal projector on
f_{0}^{+}
in (
1.15
) and
\Pi^{\angle}:=\mathrm{Id}-\Pi_{0}^{+}
.
One has

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{0}\,P_{0}=P_{0}^{*}\,{\cal B}_{0}=\Pi_{0}^{+}\,,\qquad{\cal B}_{0}\,P_{1}+{\cal B}_{1}\,P_{0}=P_{1}^{*}\,{\cal B}_{0}+P_{0}^{*}\,{\cal B}_{1}\,,

(3.31)

\displaystyle P_{0}\,P_{1}\,P_{0}=0\,,\qquad P_{0}\,P_{2}\,P_{0}=-P_{1}^{2}\,P_{0}=-P_{0}\,P_{1}^{2}\,,

(3.32)

\displaystyle(P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{2}P_{0}=P_{0}(\mathrm{Id}-P_{\mu,\epsilon})\,P_{0}\,,\qquad(P_{\mu,\epsilon}-P_{0})^{4}P_{0}=P_{0}\big((\mathrm{Id}-P_{\mu,\epsilon})P_{0}\big)^{2}\,,

(3.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}P_{j}=P_{j}^{*}\mathcal{J}\,,\ \ \forall j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,,\qquad P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{j}\Pi^{\angle}P_{0}=\Pi_{0}^{+}P_{j}\Pi^{\angle}P_{0}\,.

(3.34)

Proof.

We deduce that
{\cal B}_{0}\,P_{0}=\Pi_{0}^{+}
because

{\cal B}_{0}f_{1}^{+}={\cal B}_{0}f_{1}^{-}={\cal B}_{0}f_{0}^{-}=0
,
{\cal B}_{0}f_{0}^{+}=f_{0}^{+}

and the first identity in (
3.31
) follows also since

P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}={\cal B}_{0}P_{0}
by (
3.29
).
The second one follows by expanding the identity in (
3.29
) at order 1, using the expansions of
P_{\mu,\epsilon}
and
B_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
3.12
) and (
3.7
).
The identities in (
3.32
) follow by expanding
the identity
P_{\mu,\epsilon}^{2}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}
at order 1 and 2, getting

P_{1}P_{0}+P_{0}P_{1}=P_{1}
and

P_{2}P_{0}+P_{1}^{2}+P_{0}P_{2}=P_{2}
,
and applying
P_{0}
to the right and the left of the identities above. The first
identity in (
3.33
) is verified using that
P_{\mu,\epsilon}^{2}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}

and the second one follows by applying the first one twice. Finally the first identity in (
3.34
) follows by expanding the identity
\mathcal{J}P_{\mu,\epsilon}=P_{\mu,\epsilon}^{*}\mathcal{J}
in (
2.4
) into homogeneous orders. The last identity in
(
3.34
) descends from the first of (
3.31
), since for any
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and
f_{k}^{\sigma}\in\{f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{-},f_{0}^{-}\}
one has

\big(P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{j}f_{k}^{\sigma},g\big)=\big(P_{j}f_{k}^{\sigma},{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}g\big)=\big(P_{j}f_{k}^{\sigma},\Pi_{0}^{+}g\big)=\big(\Pi_{0}^{+}P_{j}f_{k}^{\sigma},g\big)\,.

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

By (
3.33
) and (
3.32
) the Taylor expansions of
g_{2}P_{0}
and
g_{4}P_{0}
in (
3.28
) are

\displaystyle g_{2}P_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}P_{0}(\textup{Id}-P_{\mu,\epsilon})P_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}P_{0}\,P_{2}\,P_{0}-\frac{1}{2}P_{0}\,P_{3}\,P_{0}-\frac{1}{2}P_{0}\,P_{4}\,P_{0}+\mathcal{O}_{5}\,,

(3.35a)

\displaystyle g_{4}P_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{8}P_{0}(\textup{Id}-P_{\mu,\epsilon})P_{0}(\textup{Id}-P_{\mu,\epsilon})P_{0}=\frac{3}{8}P_{0}\,P_{2}\,P_{0}\,P_{2}\,P_{0}+\mathcal{O}_{5}\,.

(3.35b)

We now Taylor expand the operators in (
3.30
) and collect the terms of the same order.

Expression of
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
:
The term of order 0 in (
3.30
) is simply
\mathfrak{B}_{0}=P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}
.

Expression of
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
:
The term of order 1 is

\mathfrak{B}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{0}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{0}+P_{1}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}+P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{1}\big)P_{0}=P_{0}^{*}\,{\cal B}_{1}\,P_{0}

using (
3.32
) and (
3.31
)
and that
{\cal B}_{0},{\cal B}_{1}
are self-adjoint.

Expression of
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
:
We compute the terms of order 2
in (
3.30
). By (
3.35a
) we get

\mathfrak{B}_{2}=\mathbf{Sym}[P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{2}P_{0}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{0}P_{2}-{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}P_{2}\big)P_{0}]\,.

(3.36)

Moreover

P_{0}^{*}\left({\cal B}_{0}P_{2}-{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}P_{2}\right)P_{0}=P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}(\textup{Id}-P_{0})P_{2}P_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B.id}}}{{=}}{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}(\textup{Id}-P_{0})P_{2}P_{0}=0\ ,

and from (
3.36
) descends
the expression of
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
in (
3.24a
).

Expression of
\mathfrak{B}_{3}
:
We compute the terms of order 3
in (
3.30
).
By (
3.35a
), identity (
3.24b
) follows from

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Sym}[P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{3}+{\cal B}_{2}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{2}+{\cal B}_{0}P_{3}-({\cal B}_{0}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{0})P_{0}\,P_{2}\,P_{0}-{\cal B}_{0}\,P_{0}\,P_{3}\,P_{0}\big)P_{0}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Sym}[P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{3}+{\cal B}_{2}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{2}-{\cal B}_{1}P_{0}P_{2}\big)P_{0}],

where we used

P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{3}=P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}P_{3}

and

P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{1}P_{0}P_{2}P_{0}=0

by (
3.31
) and (
3.32
).

Expression of
\mathfrak{B}_{4}
:

At the fourth order we get, in view of (
3.35a
) and (
3.35b
),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{4}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Sym}\big[P_{0}^{*}\Big({\cal B}_{0}P_{4}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{3}+{\cal B}_{2}P_{2}+{\cal B}_{3}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{4}-{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}P_{4}P_{0}-({\cal B}_{0}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{0})P_{0}P_{3}P_{0}

\displaystyle\quad-({\cal B}_{2}P_{0}+{\cal B}_{1}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{0}P_{2})P_{0}P_{2}P_{0}+\frac{3}{4}{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}P_{2}P_{0}P_{2}P_{0}+\frac{1}{4}P_{2}^{*}P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}P_{2}P_{0}\Big)P_{0}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Sym}\big[P_{0}^{*}\Big({\cal B}_{1}(\mathrm{Id}-P_{0})P_{3}+{\cal B}_{2}(\mathrm{Id}-P_{0})P_{2}+{\cal B}_{3}P_{1}+{\cal B}_{4}-{\cal B}_{1}P_{1}P_{0}P_{2}P_{0}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{4}{\cal B}_{0}(P_{0}P_{2}P_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{4}P_{2}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}P_{2}P_{0}\Big)P_{0}\big]\,,

(3.37)

where to pass from the first to the second line we used
P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{4}=P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{0}P_{4}
(by (
3.31
)) and

P_{0}^{*}{\cal B}_{0}P_{1}P_{0}P_{3}P_{0}=0
(by (
3.31
) and (
3.32
)).
We sum up the last two terms in (
3.37
) into
\mathbf{Sym}[P_{0}^{*}\mathfrak{N}P_{0}P_{2}P_{0}]
where

\mathfrak{N}
is in (
3.25
).
We observe that, in view of (
3.31
)-(
3.34
), we have, for any
f_{k}^{\sigma},f_{k^{\prime}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\in\{f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{-},f_{0}^{-}\}
, that (
3.26
) holds.
Thus we obtain (
3.24c
).
In conclusion, we have proved formula (
3.23
).
∎

Action of the jets of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
on the kernel vectors.

.

Lemma 3.8
.

The first jets of the operator
\mathfrak{B}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (

) act, for
f_{k}^{\sigma}\in\{f_{1}^{+},f_{1}^{-},f_{0}^{-}\}
, as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{0,2}f_{k}^{\sigma}=P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{0,2}+{\cal B}_{0,1}P_{0,1}\big)f_{k}^{\sigma}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}_{2,0}f_{k}^{\sigma}=P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{2,0}+{\cal B}_{1,0}P_{1,0}\big)f_{k}^{\sigma}\,,

(3.38a)

\displaystyle\textup{$\mathfrak{B}$}_{1,1}f_{k}^{\sigma}=P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{1,1}+{\cal B}_{1,0}P_{0,1}+{\cal B}_{0,1}P_{1,0}+\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{0}^{+}P_{1,1}\big)f_{k}^{\sigma}\,,

(3.38b)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\textup{$\mathfrak{B}$}_{0,3}f_{k}^{\sigma}=P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{0,3}+{\cal B}_{0,2}P_{0,1}+{\cal B}_{0,1}P_{0,2}-\textup{{Sym}}[{\cal B}_{0,1}P_{0}P_{0,2}]\big)f_{k}^{\sigma}\,,\\
&\textup{$\mathfrak{B}$}_{3,0}f_{k}^{\sigma}=P_{0}^{*}\big({\cal B}_{3,0}+{\cal B}_{2,0}P_{1,0}+{\cal B}_{1,0}P_{2,0}-\textup{{Sym}}[{\cal B}_{1,0}P_{0}P_{2,0}]\big)f_{k}^{\sigma}\,,\end{aligned}
